### [The great human centipede that is Twitter](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Ctia6zyXF69N3tzhv/the-great-human-centipede-that-is-twitter)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On October 29th, 2026, the New York Times reported that Jensen Huang had a ‘close relationship’ with Alice Huang, an executive U.S. officials had concluded was a Chinese spy. The story had six reporters on the byline, and was sourced to "people briefed on the surveillance." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whether or not you think Alice Huang was working for the MSS, a rational person might conclude that the "person briefed on the surveillance", who supplied the initial story, was a U.S. government official. After all, if you were not possessing a security clearance and working for U.S. government, you would not have been "briefed" on U.S. counterintelligence investigations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As the story gained traction this month, people who think quite </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">differently</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> began to propose other theories. On November 10th - the first time I can find this suggested - an account called "</span><a href="https://x.com/not_ellington/status/2120267393440326003"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ellington</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" stated their "personal conspiracy theory" that Anthropic was the New York Times' source (2k likes):</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 79.70520203927492%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794780308/lexical_client_uploads/e1vik2nehjatpmrblfsp.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few hours later GeLun Ding, a Chinese-language account that shows up again below, </span><a href="https://x.com/gelunding/status/2120337484819165383"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said the same thing without hedging</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (1.4k likes):</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 82.4773413897281%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794780774/lexical_client_uploads/medjksfgbv87iyawycuu.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 11th, 6PM Pacific Time, Dream1984Tube, another Chinese-language account </span><a href="https://x.com/Dream1984Tube/status/2120699298044088676"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that he had "found the key clue" to who is really behind the hunt for Jensen Huang, and that he was writing it up: </span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 81.41993957703929%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794782554/lexical_client_uploads/jymzztbbpwd8slitatfk.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Twenty minutes later, he </span><a href="https://x.com/Dream1984Tube/status/2120704273289711622"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">says</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> he needs to take another look at Ana Swanson, one of the six Times reporters. At 6:32 PM he </span><a href="https://x.com/Dream1984Tube/status/2120706198848614895"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">brings up Jack Clark (cofounder of Anthropic) for the first time</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, on the grounds that Clark went to an Aspen Institute dinner with one of the six reporters:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 77.79456193353475%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794782813/lexical_client_uploads/ryc0wkmcdiwbyvldu0ik.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 11th at 9:14 PM PT, he posted an AI-generated longform article on X titled "</span><a href="https://x.com/Dream1984Tube/status/2120746927100715509" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who Is Really Behind the Hunt for Jensen Huang?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" This is the first post, on or off twitter, that accuses Jack Clark of being the source behind the October 29th article, and it's the first one that takes off:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 78.54984894259819%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794766658/lexical_client_uploads/demn6o9ihvkscbwmf3kd.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most of the content is devoted to describing second- and third- degree connections between the people who talked about or expressed indignation at the affair. It contains only the following core points:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jensen Huang did not support export controls on chips, and Anthropic did.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ana Swanson is one of the six reporters on the byline for the New York Times article. Ana Swanson has quoted Jack Clark as a source in the past. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At this point in Twitter history, this is the entirety of the concrete evidence offered, that Jack Clark was the source for the October 29th New York Times article. His name has been repeated many times on X throughout the saga. As far as I can tell, no one has ever attempted to explain how Jack Clark would know who Jensen Huang is sleeping with. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grok's post is then amplified by other mouthbreathers in the tech industry, much more than the previous tweets:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 83.38368580060424%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794768882/lexical_client_uploads/lrbfinpzfqdbjfqd6gjj.png" /></figure><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And within the hour, "Hensen Juang", a South Asian twitter account, performs the standard magick whereby inferences are transmuted into observations. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He first posts a couple "suggestive" tweets:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 88.67069486404834%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794773362/lexical_client_uploads/a3baptxfy0qttsijfv7n.png" /></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 88.51963746223565%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794773429/lexical_client_uploads/up7qyubjstkuqviqjtsd.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then, in his most popular tweet, he restates the main insinuation from the article without qualification, and receives thousands of likes:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794769033/lexical_client_uploads/dbwdnibdindcxhcqthc9.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pulled from Twitscan, which shows UTC (9:52 PM PT)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An hour and a half after posting, he adds a one-word reply consisting of the word "Allegedly", which gets an order of magnitude less circulation than the original tweet. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And a day or two later after that he deletes the original tweet, after about a million people have already seen it:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 88.51963746223565%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794769088/lexical_client_uploads/jkuqwbs11zzfeilyto3m.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The rumor continues to spread, in various formats, mostly across other Chinese language accounts (screenshots cropped to fit on the page, so that you can see tweet metrics):</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.91540785498489%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794774626/lexical_client_uploads/ftvgrdw3iqhuchh5dsoz.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.79621412386706%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794774687/lexical_client_uploads/ihdtjvi68vyb9jbyd4ab.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Simultaneously, a game of telephone begins. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During a </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=raFEJooD9UA" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">podcast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Tripp Mickle discusses how the New York Times first started looking into MegaSpeed last year, for an </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2025/10/09/technology/nvidia-chips-china-megaspeed.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">article</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> published on October 9th, 2025 titled '</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is China Buying Banned Nvidia Chips?</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">':</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Natalie Kitroeff:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Okay, so how do we get to this place where one of the most powerful CEOs in the world gets tangled up in such an explosive, high-stakes mess? Like, where does this story begin?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tripp Mickle:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You have to back up to last August [2025] when Ana Swanson and I got a message from a tech industry contact of ours who said, “Hey, you really need to drop everything and look at this company called Megaspeed.” It’s a Singaporean data center firm. I mean, it’s literally a company that buys chips from Nvidia and builds data centers. And there’s nothing about that on the surface that says this is something we should drop what we’re doing and devote time and resources to figuring out what this company is and why it matters.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To re-emphasize some relevant context here:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This describes a "tip", not a "source". </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This tip was from last year, before U.S. intelligence knew about the affair.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The tip was what began the investigation for the October </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2025</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> article, about how MegaSpeed was being scrutinized by U.S. officials. It wasn't about Jensen personally. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He never says the "tip" is from someone affiliated with an AI lab, just that they're a "tech industry contact." One could just as easily suppose that the tip came from someone working on or close to the deal. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Context notwithstanding, here is how Philippe Lemoine reports this interaction on X The Everything App, at 6am on November 12th:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.00906344410876%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794783959/lexical_client_uploads/isilnlekgdj88gcldmcl.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 86.85800604229608%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794783977/lexical_client_uploads/ur4d9pqfqowosmkwgdab.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Philippe's tweet heavily implies that "the story" that "didn't originate with the intelligence community" is the one that kickstarted Jensen's firing. A normal reader would incorrectly assume that a "tech industry contact" is who told the New York Times about Jensen's affair. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The next day, Chief Nerd, an account with nearly a million followers, cites the same clip but this time claims that the "Jensen Huang story" was the one tipped off by the "tech industry contact:"</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.36253776435045%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794784025/lexical_client_uploads/q9sqauizgqvtsdtqax1n.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And about this time is when Chinese language accounts then transmute "a tech industry contact" into "Anthropic", in order to get an order of magnitude more uptake:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 81.2688821752266%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794783227/lexical_client_uploads/p9denmonsa06bqkdnb8k.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 81.72205438066466%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794783275/lexical_client_uploads/aiqyf4vb8r12c1glbsmw.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post, by "Dragonhau66", is what is finally screenshotted and converted back into English language by other Chinese-speaking users. During the translation, "Anthropic" is transmuted further into specifically "Jack Clark, co founder of Anthropic":</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.66465256797582%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794778833/lexical_client_uploads/muunfjrow0d7zbdfe4ft.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And this is when the "story" really takes off:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 82.93051359516616%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794785619/lexical_client_uploads/hg70cll9gb2pcmrfuq4n.png" /></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 82.87150679758308%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794768350/lexical_client_uploads/jt28otvaepaliehydhop.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"And what do you know, the person who leaked the “honeypot” story to the press was none other than ANTHROPIC CO-FOUNDER Jack Clark, who reportedly holds a larger stake in Anthropic than Dario Amodei himself…"</span></p><p><br /></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 82.62839879154077%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794786819/lexical_client_uploads/owehieib9ml6sugaveem.png" /></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.21148036253777%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794787642/lexical_client_uploads/txt4xfvzd4g0zlmfzmnc.png" /></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.20793995468279%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794787874/lexical_client_uploads/v0rcfgck2mvsiiskck1x.png" /></figure><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.26340634441088%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794787826/lexical_client_uploads/xsqjnvk2crb29qapw7xe.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's probably worth noting that back in the land of legacy media, Marco Rubio </span><a href="https://archive.is/oZ6l7#selection-489.15-489.98" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">was already named as the author of the memo warning Trump about the relationship between Huang and a suspected Chinese spy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. According to the Washington Post:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rubio used the CIA report to write his own, recasting the agency’s reporting in his own more assertive terms,</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the person said, speaking on the condition of anonymity to describe confidential intelligence.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Post article describes the memo's authorship as part of a power struggle within the executive branch, over whether Nvidia should be allowed to sell chips to China. I don't think Rubio leaked the memo, but if people feel like being conspiratorial, it seems much more plausible that he circulated the memo knowing there was a chance it would be leaked.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is now Sunday and the story is still ongoing; I saw people casually mentioning the "Anthropic coup" while doing research to write this post, and I suspect I will continue to see references to Jack Clark's treachery for months to come. Doing this trace took way more time and energy for me than it did for these individuals to write their tweets in the first place, and my trace will not spread nearly as far. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't really care about the particular topic, or about Jack Clark. I only take the time to discuss it as a way of showcasing how information travels on Twitter in the modal case, and to gesture at the hundreds of other well-circulated bits of false information I've observed, which no one will bother to "debunk" in some visible way. If you use Twitter extensively to keep up with AI, especially if you tell yourself that you're "protecting" yourself against the malicious influence of traditional media, you should assume some substantial portion of your worldview is built on lies. </span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Ctia6zyXF69N3tzhv/the-great-human-centipede-that-is-twitter#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Why so many arguments about human nature?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JoCHxFZDQuBXZAm3t/why-so-many-arguments-about-human-nature)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leans fairly heavily on: </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/duvzdffTzL3dWJcxn/believing-in-1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Believing In</span></a></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: Straightforward, I think, but could use checking.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People have long had heated arguments about various aspects of human nature, such as</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In what ways is or isn’t it human nature to care about others?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are humans innately monogamous or polyamorous?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is it natural to us to sleep for eight hours in a block at night, or to have some particular other rhythm?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and piles of others.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would like to enter these arguments soon, by arguing that in a particular sense we are not “optimizers,” and giving a model of what I think we are instead. But before that, in this post, I’d like to discuss why such arguments get so heated to begin with.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The heatedness of these arguments used to confuse me, because:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If there is “a” human nature, figuring out what it is should maybe be a straightforward matter for pooling data, rather than for politics?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If there isn’t “a” human nature, what are people even arguing about? </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Either way, maybe the amount of politics around this question ought to indicate that it is “off” in some way, and if so, why do people keep arguing about it instead of noticing that it is “off” and losing interest?</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humans vary by context</span></h1><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Nature, Mr. Allnut, is what we are put in this world to rise above.”</span></i><br /><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– Katharine Hepburn, playing Rose Sayer, in the movie The African Queen</span></i></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794794922/lexical_client_uploads/dsyxg0fyu0lz34ttlx8l.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This rock has a shape. I can rotate it, but it retains its shape.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794794922/lexical_client_uploads/dhiu4cmoub1bgvsmjmim.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshimoto_Cube"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yoshimoto Cube</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a toy that can be arranged into many different shapes. These shapes are intentionally designed into its set of possibilities. It does not make sense to ask what this toy’s shape is (in the singular), but it does make sense to ask what range of shapes it can occupy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humans seem to me more like the toy than like the rock: we are evolved to be configurable. That is, we are evolved to be deeply influenced by our culture, our early experiences, and our surroundings, in ways that can be relatively stable across adult life in a particular context. Thus, it is for many purposes better to ask what range of natures we humans can easily and stably take on (individually and in groups), rather than looking only for a single “most natural” human configuration.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beliefs about “human nature” are part of what shapes us</span></h1><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“There's a picture in your head… and you do whatever that picture would do, whether or not it makes any sense.” – HPMOR, ch 90</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How exactly do particular humans end up in one particular human configuration, from among the many possible?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Awkwardly, part of it is that many of us are more likely to take an action that fits with:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The models of ourselves that we earlier </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/duvzdffTzL3dWJcxn/believing-in-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">believed in</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (planned around; bet resources as though those models would hold); and</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The models of us, and of humans broadly, that our communities earlier </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/duvzdffTzL3dWJcxn/believing-in-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">believed in</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, a person Bob who grows up in a society where almost everybody is monogamous, and where almost everybody is planning around the assumption that Bob will be monogamous, is more likely to plan their life around the assumption that they’ll be monogamous and is more likely to in fact make monogamous choices.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffw7ibn7pa2s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfw7ibn7pa2s">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, part of what will (sensibly) cause a person or society to believe in (plan around) a particular model of human nature is whether they predict that that model will “take” (whether people are likely to act stably like that, once primed), as well as whether they think that nature is “good” (whether good things will follow if people act like that). That causes an incentive to mess with other people’s predictions about human nature, in order to shape people’s nature.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Insofar as shared community predictions about human nature have important effects on how we act, it makes sense that some would wish to do politics about “what human nature is.” </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Separately, of course, human nature is not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">entirely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> shaped by our beliefs about ourselves. Reality will peek out from behind our models.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A role for knowledge, political choice, and personal choice</span></h1><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“You decide what’s right</span></i><br /><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You decide what’s good</span></i><br /><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No one is alone…” – lyrics from Sondheim’s “Into the Woods”</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given the above, I would personally like to aim for all of the following at once – and I believe it’s coherent to aim for all of these at once:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’d like accurate models of the range of configurations humans can and cannot occupy</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">… and of how easy it is to get us into each of these configurations, how stable they are, etc</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This falls under “knowledge” or “true beliefs”</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’d like to be in a community where there’s lots of support for human configurations that I and others want to be in</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pulling this off, and deciding what configurations those are exactly, falls under “political choice”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some communities would undertake this negotiation by attempting to distort one another’s predictions; but I aspire to be in communities that keep our predictions and our </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/duvzdffTzL3dWJcxn/believing-in-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">believing ins</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> well-factored.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’d like to personally occupy a human configuration that can help me do neat things, have a good life, etc</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, this requires knowledge: which configurations, exactly, are likely to “take,” in me? Which will have good effects?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, it requires personal choice (what exactly do I want?)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And also, the thing Eliezer </span><a href="https://yudkowsky.tumblr.com/post/103192100330/cognitive-trope-therapy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> calls “author power” – the ability to act coherently from a chosen identity, including with my more visceral processes.</span></li></ul></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the politics that surrounds many discussions on “human nature” is evidence this factoring-out must be done carefully, but I think it’s possible. As noted earlier, I intend soon to write a post arguing that humans “are not” optimizers, and that that there’s a particular other sort of thing we are more usefully modeled as instead. When I make that post, I’ll be trying to keep carefully separate: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arguments this alternative model offers more accurate predictions, even about people who think they're optimizers; and</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">arguments that this model may be a desirable political and/or personal choice for other reasons.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Separately: while it’s its own conversation with room for subtleties, there are analogies between the above discussion of “human nature” and questions about what “LLM nature” is (or what “Claude’s nature” is, what “Astra’s nature” is, etc). (Points of analogy: like humans, LLMs can probably occupy a range of patterns depending on context. Also, these creatures are shaped partly by their own predictions about themselves, which are in turn shaped partly by stories about them in their pre-training corpus, and these stories are in turn shaped partly by the choices and beliefs of humans and LLMs together; so there is room for partially self-fulfilling prophecies, and room also for reality to peek out from behind models. And all of that does not IMO mean that e.g. lying to try to seed better self-fulfilling prophecies is a good idea; I want arguments about what to predict, and arguments about what to believe in, to be voiced separately.)</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfw7ibn7pa2s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffw7ibn7pa2s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re wondering why I expect this to be true of Bob, and why I more generally expect a “partially self-fulfilling prophecy” feedback from how people expect themselves and others to work, to how they do work: there are several reasons. First, it intuitively seems to fit my experiences with myself and others, e.g. the Bob story rings true to me. Second, predictive processing claims something like this, and I would guess predictive processing pays some rent though I’d be shocked if it’s fully true. Separately, there is often upside in acting this way, as it allows the actor to be more often in planned-for situations, and also as it is often the case that the reason I (or whoever) expected myself to take action A is that A has various upsides. See also Abram’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/inedT6KkbLSDwZvfd/steam#Steam_as_Action_Probability"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steam as action probability</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JoCHxFZDQuBXZAm3t/why-so-many-arguments-about-human-nature#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Giving Up on Saving the World](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvDpcoLqpHvQEmx8B/giving-up-on-saving-the-world)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>When I was 19, I decided I was going to save the world.</p><p>Now, 25 years later, I’ve decided to give up.</p><p>To explain why and what giving up means, we first need to go back to how I ever got into the world-saving business. As a kid, I loved computers, and I started teaching myself to program them when I was 8. By 16 I could write real programs that did interesting things, and it was also around this time I got into Linux. And since I was into Linux, that meant I read <a href="https://slashdot.org/">Slashdot</a>.</p><p>If you weren’t into Linux in the late 90s, you might not even know what Slashdot was. It still exists, but it’s a shell of its former self. Back then it was a vibrant, community-driven news site that in many important ways felt to me like LessWrong feels now. And it was from Slashdot that I learned about nanotech and grey goo, which led me to the Foresight Institute, which led me to the Extropians, which ultimately led to me being a very early member of the SL4 mailing list.</p><p>And, again, if you’re not familiar with SL4, it’s approximately how all this AI safety and rationalist stuff got started. And so it was sometime over the summer of 2001, discussing Friendly AI with Eliezer Yudkowsky on IRC, that I decided to orient my life around saving the world from the risks of superintelligent AI.</p><p>The only problem was, I wasn’t up to the task yet. In fact, nobody was. In that first decade I, along with everyone else, was just trying to figure out how to think well enough to be able to think usefully about AI at all. It proved a surprisingly hard task, and one that required the founding of the rationalist community.</p><p>While all this was happening, I was also living my life. I stayed in school for a while trying for a math PhD, then dropped out, joined a startup, and got married. When the startup and the marriage failed, I took the chance to move to the Bay Area. This was in 2013, and the rationalist community was starting to come together in person. I moved into a rationalist group house in Berkeley, took a job with another startup, and on nights and weekends worked on my “hobby” of figuring out how to save the world.</p><p>Over time, I came up with some ideas. <a href="https://paisri.org/">I wrote a lot about them</a>. I still think some of them are pretty good ideas that are worth exploring more. They might even contribute to world saving. But by 2020 it was clear to me I was less interested in AI safety engineering (at least as it existed at that time) and more interested in philosophy, so I pivoted to writing <em><a href="https://fundamentaluncertainty.com/">Fundamental Uncertainty</a></em>, a book about all the philosophy I’d had to work out to think clearly about AI. My hope was still that I’d save the world, but I’d do it by enabling other people to work effectively in AI safety. I finished that book earlier this year, and then the question became, what should I do next?</p><p>Life conspired for a while to keep me from answering that question. I suffered my fourth concussion in July and have had post-concussion syndrome since then. I’m close to being back to normal, but still not completely there. I also took on a larger role at <a href="https://www.withpulley.com/">work</a>, and while I think what we’re doing is important, it’s obviously not saving the world from AI-induced catastrophe. So between concussion recovery and work, I’ve not had a lot of time to do anything extra, and in that time the world has also changed.</p><p>The timeline to superintelligence is looking short. At the moment, the most likely world-saving activities are in policy and politics, two areas I’ve long shied away from. I do what I can. I write and call my representatives. I engage on Twitter. I do my best to be a link in preference cascade chains that may lead to effective policy outcomes.</p><p>But between the changes in my life and changes in the world, I’ve had to accept that I’m not going to be the one to save the world, and it’s time I stopped trying. And that’s okay, because it’s not up to one person to save it. Reality isn’t like a Marvel movie or a fairytale. The future of the world doesn’t hang—<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanislav_Petrov">or only does very rarely</a>—on the actions of a single person. Instead, it’s the actions of many people working together that have saved the world. That’s how we managed to keep the ozone layer, stop acid rain, prevent nuclear war, engineer our way out of Y2K, and much else.</p><p>And so I think I can finally say, after spending more than half my life trying to save the world, that I’ve given up. Not on the world being saved, but on saving the world on my own.</p><p>I want to be clear that I do still think we can save the world, and that the world needs saving, specifically from the threat posed by superintelligent AI. It’s just that such saving is unlikely to happen because one person did one thing, but instead because many people did many things to avert myriad potential catastrophes. So although I’m giving up on saving the world, I’m not giving up on the world being saved, and I’ll do what I can to contribute to that effort.</p><div><div><div><p></p></div><div><div></div><div></div></div></div></div><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvDpcoLqpHvQEmx8B/giving-up-on-saving-the-world#comments">Discuss</a>
