### [Easier To Be Good](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4zbvagnfFsJbkSxij/easier-to-be-good)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do not consider myself a good person. I am lazy and frivolous and inconsiderate, I care too much about intelligence and status, I am a snob and a hypocrite, and I do not call my mother enough.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't mean this to read as self-hatred, nor a show of false modesty. Among all the people I know there is only a scant handful of people I would consider genuinely good, and so it is unremarkable to me that I wouldn't find myself amongst their number.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You do not need to be good to desire more goodness in the world. It is good for everyone when things are good, and when there is more of it — more thoughtfulness, more generosity, more coordination, more care, more beauty, more clarity, more positive sum trades, more sophistication and elegance in our solutions and resolutions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Increasing the amount of goodness in the world is an urgent project. The world </span><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/much-better-awful-can-be-better"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really sucks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> right now, and even in the wealthiest enclaves of the imperial core there is an immense amount of </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2015/12/24/how-bad-are-things/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">suffering</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To reach the healed world would be a massive complicated undertaking. It is such a massive complicated undertaking that it is not enough for only those who are intrinsically good to work on it. There simply aren’t enough of them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If it is desirable (as I contend) for morally mediocre people like me to work on the project, it would not be enough to appeal to their innate goodness, for they have not that much of it to be appealed to. You will need to offer me things that are more base, like status and money and the affirmation that I am a good person (for everyone </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wants</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to be a virtuous person and to be recognized as such, whether or not they are).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fortuitously, there was an instructive breakthrough around a decade ago: effective altruism. Here's approximately what that is: you take people who come from the morally bankrupt culture that is the western professional managerial class, and you give us the prestige and status and affirmation of virtue we neurotically crave for the trivial cost of ten percent of our income and a few weekends a year where we are subject to vegan food and like sixteen hours of one-on-one discussions with a bunch of earnest dorks. As a result, suddenly a contingent of this culture begins to compete in a new arena of QALYs and utilons and eyestalk ablation literature reviews rather than the traditional arena of summer home square footage and the number of Michelin star restaurants one has set foot in.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Put it that way, and it doesn't sound so different from the beautiful promise of capitalism: that if we can successfully align the system with human values, it will result in some sort of elegant virtuous cycle such that people can go about selfishly pursuing their self-interested goals, and end up pouring immense amounts of goodness into the world as a side product.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a mediocre person, it does sure seem self-evident to me that the only way to get mediocre people to do good, en masse, is to generate more selfish incentives for doing so. Whether capitalism has successfully done that is discoursebait I will leave to more foolhardy poasters.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffbybduy316g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfbybduy316g">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> What I know </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for sure</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is that effective altruism did this for me. I dedicate much more of my energy and my money towards the reduction of suffering of sentient beings than I otherwise would, by a really really really wide margin.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It did this by not only appealing to some of my best instincts, but also some of my ugly ones, and ones that are simply mammalian. The community gave me peers that respected me and pushed me towards more virtuousness. I have recognition for my talents, and social status, and the occasional extra </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">bag of money</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> bit of funding when I do things that are unusually aligned with their goals. Insofar as engaging in effective altruism makes me do more improving things for the world than I would otherwise, it seems wise to continue to engage with it: to reap all of the rewards and allow myself to enjoy them to the fullest without guilt, such that I have as much motivation as I can get to continue to do good things for the world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am impure, so perhaps it is unsurprising that I've always disliked purity in my politics. Stringent, inhuman standards do not seem to me like the most effective way to increase the amount of goodness in the world. More specifically, stringent, inhuman standards will get you the small minority of humans who are inhumanly virtuous, and leave the rest of us out. To me that feels like leaving resources on the table.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am not a good person, but I am a better one than I was before people started paying me in tangibles and intangibles to act like one. I would like as many people as possible to experience this privilege. For that to be the case, we will need to be thoughtful about how to bring more people into the fold — how to create a culture in which it is easier for people to be good.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfbybduy316g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffbybduy316g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...And my footnotes. I recognize that neither capitalism or the effective altruist ecosystem is as well aligned as its ideal versions would be, but this does not overly trouble me. Alignment problems of all kinds are </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wicked_problem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wicked</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and difficult, and it makes sense to me that the very complicated ones (that won’t kill us imminently) might take up to centuries to straighten out. As long as there is continual improvement, I feel okay with the slowness of the bend towards justice.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4zbvagnfFsJbkSxij/easier-to-be-good#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Alignment 'train/test splits' should be sacred](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FX2xcrsqBYonLfoge/alignment-train-test-splits-should-be-sacred)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tl;dr: Alignment techniques can be used for training alignment or for testing it, but they cannot be used for both! As we build out the set of alignment techniques available to us, we should enforce that some are judiciously reserved only for evaluating alignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See also: </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CXYf7kGBecZMajrXC/validating-against-a-misalignment-detector-is-very-different"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Validating against a misalignment detector is very different to training against one</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is a guiding principle regarding alignment-minded AI training: whatever you do, you must maximally avoid teaching the model to take misaligned actions which are invisible to the user. In fact, you should be shamed if you use training techniques which encourage covert misalignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is a separate, guiding principle of machine learning: whatever you do, you must maximally avoid training the model on the entire dataset rather than holding out a test split. In fact, you should be shamed if you report results which are obtained by training on the entire dataset rather than on the train split.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I want to argue that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">these are the same principle. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training against all of your alignment assessment techniques leads to the same failure as training on your validation data in traditional machine learning: you potentially get better performance, but you sacrifice all ability to verify your model’s behavior.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider two types of alignment techniques:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some techniques </span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sculpt</span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the model’s alignment. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This includes techniques such as:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steering and other inference-time interventions</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SFT generalization techniques (midtraining, pretraining filters)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RL generalization techniques (RLHF, RLAIF, beneficial RL, GRAFT, inoculation prompting)</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prompt engineering</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inference time safety classifiers</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some techniques </span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assess</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the model’s alignment</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (aka, monitoring). This includes techniques such as:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Human analysis of model outputs</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CoT monitoring</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Activation monitoring</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Broader interp tools like SAEs, transcoders, etc.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evals for whether models can control their activations</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pre-fill analysis</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Honeypots</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Universal jailbreaks (to study what the model will do at the limit)</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of these methods for sculpting a model's alignment can be repurposed for assessing its alignment, and vice versa. To give a few illustrative examples:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chain of thought:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can train models to not verbalize scheming by training against CoT monitors</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can use CoT monitors to tell whether models are scheming</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Human feedback:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can RLHF models to behave according to human preferences</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can hold out these human analyses to tell whether the model is behaving in ways people like.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Probes</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can train against deception probes to control the model’s actions</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can deploy models with constitutional classifiers</span></li></ol></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Honeypots</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can train models to avoid honeypots</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can hold out honeypots to see whether the model takes them</span></li></ol></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Automated audits</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can use automated auditing agents (petri) to find behaviors to RL away</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can keep automated audits as a way to evaluate alignment</span></li></ol></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same can be said for gradient routing, RLAIF, data filtering, etc.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As soon as you train against the monitor (CoT, human feedback, probes, honeypots), you necessarily sacrifice this monitor's utility. The model now never admits misalignment which this monitor can catch, since many of the cases which were caught were trained away!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This holds trivially for CoT and honeypots, but it also applies to all the other alignment techniques. For instance, RLHF relies on human ‘monitors’ to catch bad responses so you can train them away. Filtering pretraining data relies on ‘monitors’ to identify samples that might cause bad generalization and then taking them out of the data mix.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the limit—where you use all of the monitors for training and leave none for the test-set—you end up at extreme ‘</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you get what you measure</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’ problems: your model will necessarily appear aligned… because you’ve trained against everything which you might use to evaluate alignment!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The best (and only) example I know of where we’ve truly tried to hold out an alignment technique is chain of thought monitorability. However, CoT monitorability is dying and it would be exceedingly unwise to end up with </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">no</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> alignment techniques in our test set.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Importantly, the dataset of alignment techniques and the dataset of alignment environments is independent: we can train/test all of our alignment techniques using just a single alignment environment. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider a single standard reward-hacking honeypot. Within this one setting, one can train the model’s alignment by: training out verbalized scheming in the CoT, updating the model’s behavior based on human feedback, training the model’s behavior against a probe, RL-ing the model to avoid the honeypot, and training against automated audits seeded on this environment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a result, we essentially have two alignment datasets. We have some set of alignment samples: these are essentially the standard notion of data samples from machine learning. But we also have a set of alignment techniques: these are the way in which we update the model’s alignment using any one sample.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My point is this: </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">models generalize across both alignment techniques and alignment samples</span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We hold out alignment samples all the time, but have significantly worse hygiene about train/test splits for alignment techniques.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyfkltvrh94i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyfkltvrh94i">[1]</a></sup></span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To operationalize the above ideas, consider </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/graft/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this recent paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> out of the Anthropic MATS stream.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In it, they propose a gradient routing mechanism—GRAFT—for removing misaligned behavior from a model. Their technique works by taking monitored (somewhat toy) RL envs and checking for reward hacks programmatically. If the hack occurred, you route the gradient update to a “Forget” adapter; otherwise, the update is routed to both the “Forget” adapter and a separate “Retain” adapter. With some optimization tricks, they show that you can then remove the Forget adapter during inference and (a) maintain good performance on the task while (b) observing less reward hacking.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before going into my diatribe, I will emphasize that I like the idea of gradient routing and that I think it is good to explore this further as an alignment technique. I think it is better to have it than to not have it at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But my point is that it could work very well as an alignment </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">testing</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> technique. That is, instead of removing the adapter responsible for reward hacking, one can simply keep this adapter and, during inference, see whether the model routes computations through it. This would allow us to catch the model misbehaving.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, this seems to have not been explored! If indeed GRAFT generalizes well to unseen reward hacks and this monitor absorbs all the ‘reward hackiness’ in the model, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we should consider relegating it to exclusively being an alignment testing technique</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, in either case, my more principled objection is that this does not solve the fundamental problem of alignment generalization. Regardless of whether you train against the monitor or you route gradients based on a monitor, you nonetheless have to rely on what the monitor can catch! And by removing the misalignments that this monitor observes, you render that monitor obsolete (e.g., you shrink your test set).</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A natural objection is: “if we have methods for assuring alignment, why shouldn’t we train models using all of them? Won’t this make our models more aligned than if we didn’t?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the one hand, yes – training on your entire dataset will give you a better model than training on some subset of that data. But training on the entire dataset also renders you unable to determine if your resulting model is fundamentally flawed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By not having a test-set, you are relegated to identifying alignment via trial-and-error during deployment. However, in the AGI regime, we cannot rely on alignment by trial and error. Indeed, AI alignment is the scientific discipline where we must respect the train/test split the most. ASI alignment has the property where our ‘ability to understand the model’ is directly commensurate with the extinction of the human race.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What does it mean to be better about this?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, we must require transparency from frontier AI companies about which, if any, alignment techniques are relegated for testing. For instance, I’d love to be able to answer the following simple question: “what kinds of alignment RL do the frontier AI companies use”? Are they just training against every misalignment they can identify, in every way they possibly can? Or is there at least one clean, truly-held-out set of alignment environments </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> techniques which the model has never seen, where we can actually evaluate the model’s behavior? </span><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/1899172712297415057"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This won’t solve everything</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but it would be way better than what we currently have.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, we should have a culture where we try to determine whether an alignment technique is better suited towards alignment training or alignment testing. For instance, RLHF seems quite good at alignment sculpting while CoT monitoring seems inherently alignment-assurance flavored. Some are less clear – for instance, it seems like people are </span><a href="https://x.com/maksym_andr/status/2106442064812523529"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">training</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> against </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.com/research/rlfr"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">activation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> monitors, but it also seems </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.15515"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inherently</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> risky </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.36490"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> do </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11949"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">so</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In cases where it’s unclear, we should prioritize studying whether this is a train-set or a test-set idea.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third, we should </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maximize</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> our set of alignment techniques and prioritize that a large portion be sequestered for test purposes. This is why adopting looped transformer architectures is such a profoundly terrible decision – not only does it remove a valuable alignment technique, it got rid of one which was assurance-shaped!</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Sam Martin, Dewi Gould, Tim Hua, Jonathan Bostock, Mikael Høgsgaard and Moritz Pfau for comments &amp; discussion. Thanks also to Raymond Douglas and Daniel Tan for lightly pressuring me to finally post this.</span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyfkltvrh94i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyfkltvrh94i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Perhaps I'm belaboring the point here, but I really feel this strongly!</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FX2xcrsqBYonLfoge/alignment-train-test-splits-should-be-sacred#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Reading Speculations Concerning the First Ultraintelligent Machine](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uKPJwHiSiweKWJ9rz/reading-speculations-concerning-the-first-ultraintelligent)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>

In 1964 [1], </span>

<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._J._Good">I. J. Good</a>
wrote


<a href="https://exhibits.stanford.edu/feigenbaum/catalog/gz727rg3869">Speculations
Concerning the First Ultraintelligent Machine</a>.  I've only ever
seen it cited for a single paragraph:



</p><p>

</p>

<blockquote>
Let an ultraintelligent machine be defined as a machine that can far
surpass all the intellectual activities of any man however clever.
Since the design of machines is one of these intellectual activities,
an ultraintelligent machine could design even better machines; there
would then unquestionably be an "intelligence explosion," and the
intelligence of man would be left far behind ...  Thus the first
ultraintelligent machine is the <i>last</i> invention that man need
ever make, provided that the machine is docile enough to tell us how
to keep it under control.  It is curious that this point is made so
seldom outside of science fiction.  It is sometimes worthwhile to take
science fiction seriously.
</blockquote>



<p>

This is becoming increasingly relevant, with the AI Futures Project
now estimating <a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q35-timelines-update-looks-like-the">an
intelligence explosion starting in 2027</a> in response to <a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/">recent
AI progress</a> <a href="https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/">in
automating</a> <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/recursive-self-improvement">AI
research</a>.  Making our 'last invention' relinquishes our influence
over the future, we have to get it right, and with the current frantic
pace disastrous mistakes are likely.

</p>

<p>

I think the appropriate reaction to this news is something like "this
is completely irresponsible, you're way too likely to get us all
killed, do not do that" and <a href="https://www.technologyreview.com/2026/03/02/1133814/i-checked-out-londons-biggest-ever-anti-ai-protest/">marching</a>
<a href="https://www.sfchronicle.com/tech/article/san-francisco-ai-protest-22340835.php">in
the</a> <a href="https://hardlyworking1.substack.com/p/protest-review-san-franciscos-ai">streets</a>
(hence <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/protesting-for-ai-regulation">yesterday's
protest attendance</a>).  But as someone who is very interested in how
people process ideas and what they thought with the information they
had at the time, my reaction also includes wanting to know more about
what Good expected six decades ago.

</p>

<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/ij-good-paper-header-big.png"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/uKPJwHiSiweKWJ9rz/ic7w2kkiuivd92aklj18" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>

<p>

I spent this morning reading his paper and talking about it with
Claude (Fable 5.2).  I was curious how the ideas had held up, what he
got right, and what he got wrong.  Some of his ideas were very
technical, and not being an ML expert I can't add much there; I've put
these in a Claude-authored <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/reading-speculations-concerning-the-first-ultraintelligent-machine#appendix">appendix</a>.

</p>

<p>

The first sentence reads very differently now:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
The survival of man depends on the early construction of an
ultraintelligent machine.
</blockquote>



<p>

I think he was saying that he was worried that without one we'd kill
ourselves off (later he writes "it will give the human race a good
chance of surviving indefinitely").  This was during the Cold War, a
couple years after the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis"> Cuban
Missile Crisis</a>, and before any agreement to limit (let alone
reduce) nuclear stockpiles.

</p>

<p>

But now I'd interpret it the other way around: our survival depends on
'early construction' the same way that a driver's survival depends
on how quickly they drive.

</p>

<p>

Reading further, the most-quoted paragraph still jumps out, and not
just through familiarity.  Reading it closely, the "provided that the
machine is docile enough to tell us how to keep it under control" is
actually pretty prescient: he's essentially describing the plan of
record, as much as there is one.  Have AI research how to align AI.
(This might work!  But it also might not.)

</p>

<p>

He's also uncertain about whether it will go well.  In the famous
paragraph above he has a "provided that the machine is docile enough",
later he says "the sign is uncertain" (math-speak for "could be good
or bad"), and speculates whether "the human race will become
redundant".  He does seem overall optimistic, though, both in the
'survival depends' quote (above) and a few other places.  For example,
"the machines will create social problems, but they might also be able
to solve them in addition to those that have been created by microbes
and men."

</p>

<p>

A live objection at the time was that there wasn't any reason to make
AI, and he cites B. V. Bowden's view that "it is easier to construct
human brains by the usual method" before responding:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
This shows that highly intelligent people can overlook the
"intelligence explosion."  It is true that it would be uneconomical to
build a machine capable <i>only</i> of ordinary intellectual
attainments, but it seems fairly probable that if this could be done
then, at double the cost, the machine could exhibit ultraintelligence.
</blockquote>



<p>

I'd actually count this as a bit of a miss: a machine capable of
human-level work that was massively cheaper than humans could still be
highly economical, and that's essentially what today's deployment of
AI is.  I suspect he didn't anticipate the extent to which high
throughput would precede high intelligence.

</p>

<p>

I also don't see where his "double the cost" is coming from, and in
practice this jump is likely to be much larger.  But the critical
thing he gets right is that human-level isn't a ceiling, and in a
series of domains (games, coding, cybersecurity, math...)
we've seen AI, after spending a while at "toy" and some time at
"useful tool", quickly pass "human level" to get to something much
more capable.  I expect this keeps going.

</p>

<p>

On the technical side, most of the details are in Claude's <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/reading-speculations-concerning-the-first-ultraintelligent-machine#appendix">appendix</a>
but at a high level it seems like he was right about a lot of how we
got here.  He thought it would be "vast artificial neural circuitry"
and expected it would learn from experience and reinforcement:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
Yet a detailed knowledge of semantics might not be required, since the
artificial neural network will largely take care of it, provided that
the parameters are correctly chosen, and provided that the network is
adequately integrated with its sensorium and motorium (input and
output).  For, if these conditions are met, the machine will be able
to learn from experience, by means of positive and negative
reinforcement, and the instruction of the machine will resemble that
of a child.
</blockquote>



<p>

He also anticipated how text-based this would all be:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
Whether a machine of classical or ultraparallel design is to be the
first ultraintelligent machine, it will need to be able to handle or
to learn to handle ordinary language with great facility.  This will
be important in order that its instructor should be able to teach it
rapidly, and so that later the machine will be able to teach the
instructor rapidly.  It is very possible also that natural languages,
or something analogous to them rather than to formal logic, are an
essential ingredient of scientific imagination.  Also the machine will
be called upon to translate languages, and perhaps to generate fine
prose and poetry at high speed, so that, all in all, linguistic
facility is at a high premium.
</blockquote>



<p>

He also correctly anticipated that AIs would use traditional computers
(still then in their infancy) as tools, with coding and search:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
In any event the ultraintelligent machine might as well have a large
electronic computer at its beck and call, and also a multimillion
dollar information-retrieval installation of large capacity but of
comparatively slow speed, since these would add little to the total
cost.
</blockquote>



<p>

And he anticipated a period before AI fully surpassed humans, during
which they'd work together:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
Until an ultraintelligent machine is built perhaps the best
intellectual feats will be performed by men and machines in very
close, sometimes called "symbiotic," relationship
</blockquote>



<p>

This is how many of us work daily, including both my day job and these
blog posts.

</p>

<p>

He was also a strong chess player (<a href="https://www.chess.com/blog/simaginfan/bletchley-chess-club-more-than-just-chess-history">on
the Bletchley Chess Club</a> during the war) and suggested:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
so-called "randomized chess," in which the positions of the white
pieces on the first rank are permuted at random before the game begins
(but with the two bishops on squares of opposite colors), and then the
initial positions of the black pieces are determined by mirror
symmetry.  This gives rise to 1440 essentially distinct initial
positions and effectively removes from the game the effect of mere
parrot learning of the openings
</blockquote>



<p>

This is very close to <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chess960">Chess960</a> (though
without the extra castling work) which Bobby Fischer proposed decades
later, and I was initially pretty excited to see it.  But reading more
it turns out this was <a href="https://www.chesshistory.com/winter/extra/variants.html">a known</a>
<a href="https://en.chessbase.com/post/chess960-or-freestyle-chess-how-it-all-began">variant
going</a>
<a href="https://www.fide.com/history-of-chess960/">back centuries</a>.
Good's including it here to illustrate that what we really care about
is the reasoning component of chess, not memorizing opening lines.

</p>

<p>

Thinking about things Good got wrong, the timing initially seems like
a big one: "more probable than not ... within the twentieth century".
But he only put it at 50%, and while it has taken longer it looks like
it's going much faster than most others predicted.

</p>

<p>

A much larger miss is the cost of artificial neurons:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>
If we could raise say a hundred billion dollars we might be able to
simulate all the neurons of a brain, and of a whole man, at a cost of
ten dollars per artificial neuron.
</blockquote>



<p>

Since <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moore%27s_law">Moore's
law</a> is newer than Good's writing it's not surprising that he
didn't fully account for it (though he did expect "great advances in
microminiaturization"), but the hardware to power an artificial neuron
costs ~10,000x less than this (~$0.01 in current dollars, less if you
quantize; Good's $10 would be ~$100 today).

</p>

<p>

On the other hand, and more importantly, he's in the right ballpark on
total cost: the world has spent maybe $1T to date creating AI systems
and I expect we spend maybe that again by the end of 2027 as recursive
self-improvement increases the returns to AI R&amp;D.  Good's $100B is $1T
in current dollars, but note that he didn't expect anyone to pay this:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>But it seems unlikely that more than say [$300M then; $3B
today] would actually be forthcoming, and even this amount might be
difficult to obtain without first building the machine!</blockquote>



<p>

This is indeed how funding has worked, with investors putting in more
money in response to initial progress.  That he ended up close to
right on the ~$1T looks to me like less of a coincidence than it
appears: costs come down over time, and it's only when they're getting
into an economically feasible range that someone might actually do it.

</p>

<p>

The biggest miss, though, is about focus.  He was super early in
thinking about whether such a machine would be good for the world, but
then spends almost the entire paper on how one might be designed.
Focusing on the "how" and not the "whether" is very normal for
technical people then and now, and as an engineer I feel this pull as
well.  But it's something people have been getting wrong since the
beginning: excitement about the potential, not staring hard at the
true scale of the risk.

</p>

<p>

This paper isn't the last we have of Good's thinking, however, and in
1998 he provided a bio to the IEEE on receiving their Computer Pioneer
Award.  It doesn't seem to have been used at the ceremony or
published, but James Barrat says Good's assistant Leslie Pendleton
gave him a copy, and Barrat quotes it in his 2013 <a href="https://gizmodo.com/why-a-superintelligent-machine-may-be-the-last-thing-we-1440091472">Our
Final Invention</a>.  Note that this is Good writing about himself in
the third person:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>

"The survival of man depends on the early construction of an
ultra-intelligent machine."  Those were his words during the Cold War,
and he now suspects that "survival" should be replaced by
"extinction." He thinks that, because of international competition, we
cannot prevent the machines from taking over.

</blockquote>



<p>

How to get countries to coordinate to prevent the machines from taking
over is a key question today.

</p>

<p>

<a name="appendix"></a>
<b>Appendix: Good's technical predictions</b>

I asked Claude Fable 5.2 to write an appendix.  The judgments are its
own, and I haven't checked them:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<blockquote>

Most of Good's paper is a theory of how a neural network could store
meaning and recall it.  Below I go through its main technical claims
and say how well I think each matches how current AI systems work.  I
grade each as a strong match, a partial match, a loose match, or a
miss.

<p>

Two cautions first.  A 58-page speculative paper makes a lot of
claims, and picking out the ones that look right in hindsight flatters
it.  And Good was less of a lone voice than this list might suggest.
Neural networks were an active research area in the early 1960s: he
cites Rosenblatt's perceptron work, and builds on Hebb, Ashby, and
Shannon.  His bet was on one of the two live approaches, the one that
then went out of fashion for decades and eventually won.

</p>
<p>

<i>Learning is adjusting connection strengths.</i>  Strong match.  In
his radio-linked machine (p35, p36):

</p>
<blockquote>
The strength of the connection could be represented by the accuracy of
the tuning.  ...  "Positive reinforcement" would correspond to
improved tuning of these filters.
</blockquote>

Every connection has an adjustable strength, and learning means
changing the strengths.  That is what a "weight" is in a modern
network, and training is nothing more than adjusting weights.

<p>

<i>How the strengths get adjusted.</i>  Miss.  Good's rule (p80) is
that a connection has a small random chance of stepping up in strength
whenever it is "successfully used (i.e., contributes to the activation
or inhibition of an artificial neuron)".  This is a local rule: each
connection changes based only on its own activity.  Modern networks
are trained by backpropagation, which works out how every weight in
the network contributed to an error in the final output and adjusts
them all together.  Nothing in the paper anticipates this, and local
rules of Good's kind have not been made to work at scale.

</p>
<p>

<i>What "reinforcement" means.</i>  Partial match.  Jeff asked whether
Good's "positive and negative reinforcement" from an instructor is
what is now called reinforcement learning from human feedback.  It
does resemble it, and that technique is used to shape how current
models behave.  But most of what those models know comes from a
different source, predicting the next word across a vast amount of
text.  Good's usage is broader than reward from an instructor.  The
rule above involves no instructor, he says the machine is educated
only "partly" by reinforcement (p80), and his account of the child is
about exposure:

</p>
<blockquote>
The child later associates words with objects and activities, by
implicit statistical inference ...  At a later stage of linguistic
development, similar statistical associations are developed between
linguistic elements themselves.  (p45, p46)
</blockquote>

He has the right raw material, the statistics of which words occur
with what, and he discusses predicting words from the preceding ones
(p43, p54).  He never proposes prediction as the thing to train on.

<p>

<i>Words are known by the company they keep.</i>  Strong match, though
not original to him.

</p>
<blockquote>
The essential idea is that words can be seen to be associated not
merely because they occur frequently together, but because they both
occur frequently in conjunction with a third word  (p71)
</blockquote>

His example is that "ferry" and "fare" are related because both go
with words like "bus".  This is the basis of how modern systems
represent word meaning.  Linguists (Harris, Firth) had stated the idea
in the 1950s.  What Good adds is the argument that a machine needs it,
since machines that "rely only on frequencies" (p71) can say nothing
about combinations they haven't seen.

<p>

<i>Word vectors.</i>  Partial match.  On p52 and p53 he takes a table
of how often each word occurs in each document, takes logarithms,
subtracts a constant from each row and column, and proposes
decomposing the result so that

</p>
<blockquote>
This would associate a <i>w</i>-dimensional vector with each index
term and with each document.  The relevance of index term <i>i</i> to
document <i>j</i> could now be defined as the correlation coefficient
between the two associated vectors.
</blockquote>

This is recognizably the method later known as latent semantic
analysis (around 1990), and a relative of the word-embedding methods
of the 2010s, which were shown to be doing something close to
factoring a similar table.  Two things are missing.  He keeps as many
dimensions as there are words, where the later methods get their power
from keeping only a few hundred.  And the vectors are computed once,
not learned by a network.  He also offers it only as "another
conceivable method" and judges the computation "exceedingly great".

<p>

<i>Generalization is the central problem.</i>  Strong match on the
diagnosis, partial on the cure.

</p>
<blockquote>
One of the main difficulties in this subject is how to estimate the
probabilities of events that have never occurred.  That such
probabilities are relevant to intelligence is to be expected, since
intelligence is sometimes defined as the ability to adapt to new
circumstances.  (p32)
</blockquote>

This is still the right way to state it.  Good had worked on this
problem with Turing, and the resulting method (Good-Turing estimation)
was a standard component of statistical language models into the
2000s.  Current models don't use it.  They generalize by learning that
similar words and situations behave similarly, which is closer to his
"ferry" and "fare" idea, and to his remark (p71) that getting the
network to form abstractions is "one of the most vital problems".

<p>

<i>What comes next depends on recent context and stored
weights.</i>  Partial match.  Good's unit of thought is the
"assembly", Hebb's term for a group of neurons that fire together and
stand for a word or idea.

</p>
<blockquote>
Which assembly becomes active at the next moment must depend on the
current sensory input, the current dominant assembly, and the
currently active subassemblies.  Indirectly, therefore, it depends on
the recent assembly sequence, wherein the most recent assemblies will
have the greatest influence.  It also depends of course on the
semipermanent static storage, the "past history."  (p60)
</blockquote>

A language model has the same division: what comes next is determined
by the recent text it is looking at (its "context") and by weights
fixed during training.  Good's list of memory types (p72) puts
short-term memory in current activity and long-term memory in
connection strengths, which is the same split, and he says a long
"attention span" is "certainly essential for intelligence" (p63).  He
goes on (p66) to score each candidate next assembly as a baseline plus
a contribution from each recent one, with the highest total firing.
The last step of a language model has that form, a score for every
possible next word.  But in a modern model the contributions from
earlier words interact in complicated ways before that step, and Good
only gestures at this by saying interaction terms will be needed
(p67).  I wouldn't claim more than a family resemblance.

<p>

<i>Concepts are spread across many neurons.</i>  Strong match, shared
credit.

</p>
<blockquote>
each neuron would occur in a great many distinct circuits, and would
not be indispensable for any of them.  (p56)
</blockquote>

He contrasts this with "one concept, one neuron".  Modern networks
store concepts as patterns over many units, with each unit taking part
in many concepts, and this is a main reason their workings are hard to
read off.  The idea came from neuroscience (he cites Lashley and
Eccles).  Good's contribution is an efficiency argument for it, by
analogy with a punched-card coding scheme.

<p>

<i>Keep probabilities, not hard decisions.</i>  Strong match.  Section
3
argues that a system understanding speech should pass
along graded probabilities between its levels instead of committing to
one answer at each, because "each definite 'decision' loses
information" (p39).  Modern networks pass continuous values between
layers and only commit at the output.

</p>
<p>

<i>Rejecting a logical "canonical language".</i>  Strong match.  He
considers representing meaning by translating every statement into a
standard formal form, and concludes this would be "extremely
difficult, perhaps even logically impossible, or perhaps it would
require an ultraintelligent machine to do it!" (p41).  Large projects
later attempted roughly this and did not get far.  Relatedly, his
guess (p36, quoted in the main post) that natural language "rather
than ... formal logic" is needed for scientific imagination is a
partial match: current models do much of their reasoning by writing
out steps in ordinary language.

</p>
<p>

<i>Scale can substitute for design.</i>  Partial match.

</p>
<blockquote>
a well-designed machine of say 10<sup>9</sup> components might be
educable to ultraintelligence, but a much more carelessly designed
machine of say 10<sup>13</sup> components might be equally good.
(p62)
</blockquote>

The history of the field broadly supports this: simple methods given
more computation have repeatedly beaten cleverer ones.  But Good,
following Ashby, bases it on the hope that almost any large, richly
connected network will behave intelligently.  That turned out to be
false.  Scale paid off only once there was a training method able to
make use of it.

<p>

<i>The hard part is ape-level intelligence.</i>  Partial match.  He
says "most of the struggle" will be building "a machine with the
intelligence of an ape" (p45), because perception and basic learning
are harder than what comes after.  In favor: getting networks to learn
to see took until about 2012, and fluent language followed within a
decade.  Against: language arrived without anything like an ape's
physical competence, and robotics is still behind.

</p>
<p>

<i>Sleep and replay.</i>  Partial match.  His conjecture that sleep
serves "to replay the assembly sequences that were of greatest
interest during the day in order to consolidate them" (p80) is close
to a well-supported view in neuroscience.  In AI, replaying stored
experience, with priority to the most informative parts, is a standard
technique in systems that learn by trial and error.  It is not part of
how language models are mainly trained, so his suggestion that the
machine would "benefit from periods of comparative rest" (p72) is a
loose match at best.

</p>
<p>

<i>Understanding as economy.</i>  Loose match.  He argues that storing
meanings instead of exact wordings saves a great deal of memory, and
that this may be "the evolutionary function of meaning and
understanding" (p77).  There is a real modern parallel, since learning
to predict text well and learning to compress it are mathematically
the same task.  Good's argument is about storage, though, and I think
the connection is more suggestive than exact.

</p>
<p>

<i>Hardware.</i>  Mixed.  He was right that serial speed alone
wouldn't do.  He expected computers to become about a million times
faster than neurons and still concluded the machine "will need to be
ultraparallel" (p35), and current systems do depend on massively
parallel chips.  He was right that low power consumption "will be
valuable" (p33).  He was wrong that the neurons would be physical
devices.  They are simulated in software.

</p>
<p>

<i>The brain as blueprint.</i>  Mostly a miss.  The paper opens by
saying that to design the machine "we need to understand more about
the human brain or human thought or both", and later that brain
anatomy is "very relevant" (p63).  Current systems borrow the neuron
and the adjustable connection and almost nothing else.  Of the brain
theory itself, Hebb's assemblies remain a respected idea in
neuroscience, but the central control system Good relies on (taken
from Penfield) has been abandoned, and his own extensions haven't been
taken up.  To his credit, he says the designer will have to guess
which features of the brain matter, and he allows that the first
machine might not use a neural network after all (p78).

</p>
<p>

<i>Theory before experiment.</i>  Miss.

</p>
<blockquote>
The need for further theory is great, since, without advances in
theory, the amount of experimentation might be prohibitive.  (p64)
</blockquote>

Computation became cheap enough to experiment at enormous scale, and
the systems we have were found that way.  Theory explaining why they
work is still well behind.

<p>

<i>How much the machine needs to learn from.</i>  Miss.  He sizes the
machine's education to a person's: "A man cannot learn more than ten
million statements in a lifetime" (p36).  Current models learn much
less from each example than people do, and compensate with volume.  By
my rough estimate they are trained on something like ten thousand
times more text than a person could read in a lifetime.

</p>
<p>

<i>Overall.</i>  Good was right about the kind of thing the machine
would be: a very large, parallel neural network that learns the
meanings of words from statistics, stores them as distributed
patterns, treats generalization as the core problem, and works in
natural language with conventional computers as tools.  He was wrong,
or silent, about how such a network could actually be trained, which
is the part that took the field another fifty years.  I'd put his
reputation for foresight on these pages at least as much as on the
famous paragraph.

</p>
</blockquote>



<p>

[1] I generally see 1965, including <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._J._Good#:~:text=Good%27s%20authorship%20of%20treatises%20such%20as%20his%201965%20%22Speculations%20Concerning%20the%20First%20Ultraintelligent%20Machine%22%5B18%5D">on
Wikipedia</a> probably because of the publication date.  But I think
it's more informative to count by the date he wrote it: "The first draft of
this monograph was completed in April 1963, and the present slightly
amended version in May 1964."

  </p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uKPJwHiSiweKWJ9rz/reading-speculations-concerning-the-first-ultraintelligent#comments">Discuss</a>
