### [Not just a bystander, not anymore…](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jcoXnm6tP4zAA4gjR/not-just-a-bystander-not-anymore)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>One Saturday night this summer, I was leisurely walking down main street hand-in-hand on a date, when suddenly, I heard a commotion about 50 yards down a busy side street. It was too far away to make out what was happening or what was being said. But I figured it was fine since the side street had plenty of people walking up and down it.</p><p>Still, I couldn’t help but peer into the distance, transfixed, like a deer in the headlights.</p><p>There was an anime convention going on that weekend. Initially, my brain reasoned that a couple of cosplayers were recording a TikTok video or doing a K-pop dance. Then my brain reasoned that surely, on a crowded street, nothing serious could be going down. Right?</p><p>Yet I waited there, unmoving, alert.</p><p>My date was casually standing there eating a cookie. She innocently asked, “What do you think’s going on over there?”</p><p>I faintly heard a woman yelling. While squinting, I saw sudden, physical contact between her and a man. I dropped my date’s hand and began aggressively stomping towards them. </p><p>All I could see was red.</p><hr /><p>I used to have anger problems during my adolescence. I didn't realize that maybe I was different from other people, until one day during my freshman year of college…<br /> </p><p>…I opened the door to my apartment to find a roomful of friends. One of them stood up from the couch to give me a hug with her arms outstretched, one hand high and the other hand low. I instinctually flinched, and then leaned in to give her a hug, but she pulled back and stopped. </p><p>“I noticed that you always flinch before I give you a hug,” she said. “How come?”</p><p>I thought about it for a second, then copied her—one hand high and the other low—then nonchalantly said, “Your high hand? That’s probably the angle from which my dad hits me.”</p><p>Her face dropped. I looked around. Everyone else’s side conversations had stopped and they were all staring at me. I noticed that I was confused—did I say something weird? My friend had asked me a question and I had matter-of-factly gave the answer. I had no idea why everyone was being so weird. I inquired, “What?”</p><p>My friend, the one who got up to give me a hug, looked at me sympathetically. “Your dad hits you?”</p><p>I noticed that I was confused again. They care about that? I gestured to the group of wide-eyed people and asserted, “What, your dads never hit you?”</p><p>Everyone stayed frozen. Then my roommate on the couch softly said, “No dude. My dad’s never hit me.” Slowly, others nodded their heads in agreement.</p><p>I felt a note of disgust. I didn’t need their pity. I retorted, “My dad’s not a monster. He doesn’t just randomly hit me for no reason. Only if I do something stupid or disobey him.” Feeling satisfied at successfully defending my dad and at clearing up the matter, my satisfaction turned into an admixture of confusion and anger—they were still just staring at me in stunned silence and pity.</p><p>“Whatever.” I went into my room and shut the door.</p><hr /><p>He was kicking her. The man was kicking her. As I stomped closer to them, my anger continued to build into a white-hot fury. My fury was only slightly abated by a thought that confused me: it’s a crowded street, there’s even a group of dads walking by and laughing with each other, so why is no one doing anything? Never mind that. My anger was building into an unstoppable rage.</p><p>Thirty feet from the assaulter, I did something that I haven’t done since I was a child—I screamed at the top of my lungs:</p><p>“HEYYY!!!”</p><p>The attacker whipped his head towards me in confused fear. I stormed forward and screamed:</p><p>“GET THE <i><strong>FUCK</strong></i> AWAY FROM HER.”</p><p>I must have had the twisted face of a rabid dog; the whites of the man’s eyes shined like saucers.</p><p>The woman screamed, “He’s trying to rape me!”</p><hr /><p>After that day in college, I reflected on my friends' reactions. Really? Their dads never hit them? Then how did their dads maintain their authority if not through fear? I couldn’t fathom an alternative method. So I shrugged and wrote them off as probably being weak.</p><p>Going forward, not much changed. I continued to exert my will over my four college roommates through intimidation and by yelling at them. Throughout my freshman year of college, one-by-one, my friends stopped coming by my apartment to say hi, stopped returning my texts, and were increasingly "too busy" to hang out when I ran into them on campus. I never felt lonely. No, I was too prideful for that. All these people just sucked. I just needed to find new, better friends. But new people, for whatever reason, didn’t stick around for too long.</p><p>This went on into my sophomore year of college. It went on until the day I had a bad trip while taking psychedelic mushrooms…<br /> </p><p>…So there I was, sitting on the ground of my university squash court with my friend, and beginning to hallucinate. At first, the spotted wood of the court looked like ants marching towards me, which was amusing.</p><p>It was amusing until, suddenly, a demon materialized in my stomach, crawled up my spinal cord, and invaded my mind. The most violent, envious, prideful thoughts spewed out of me. While it was happening, I believed it was the demon saying these things, not me, therefore not my responsibility and not my problem.</p><p>The next day, however, I realized that the demon was a manifestation of my innermost insecurities. That what the demon spake wasn’t gibberish nonsense, but instead a prophecy of what was to come if I didn’t get my shit together.</p><p>That experience scared me straight. And ever since that day seven years ago, I’m very proud that I never lost control again. I never yelled at anyone ever again. Until…</p><hr /><p>The man ignored me and started kicking her again.</p><p>Incensed, I advanced towards him and roared even louder, “HEY! GET THE <i><strong>FUCK</strong></i> AWAY FROM HER.” The man saw me stomping towards him and he stuttered backwards. Perhaps he saw in my eyes that I wasn’t fucking around, that I wanted blood.</p><p>They say that in response to immense stress, people typically have one of three reactions: fight, flight, or freeze. I was ready to <i>fight</i>. But it hasn’t always been like this…</p><hr /><p>As a child, I sometimes found myself on the other side of the kitchen table, playing a game of cat &amp; mouse with my angry older brother, tears and snot streaming down my face, squealing like a cornered pig about to be slaughtered, while trying to avoid my brother’s grasp. For I knew if he caught me, he would wrestle me to the floor, pin my arms underneath him by sitting on my torso, and then rain blows down upon me.</p><p>Over the years, after repeatedly being hit by my father, by my mother, and by my brother, I learned that <i>flight</i> was not a good strategy for me. I was the youngest and the weakest. I was never going to be able to outrun them or outfight them. Instead, I figured out that it was far better to <i>freeze</i>, study what triggered them, and then learn to tiptoe around those landmines. </p><p>And this worked. I learned to suppress my emotions while living with my family. It was the only way to assure my safety.</p><p>When I moved away to college, I turned into a monster. All my pent-up anger was unleashed upon my innocent roommates and friends. And it felt good. Fuck, it felt <i>so good </i>to lean in to my newfound rage. I got to feel powerful for once. I got to feel in control. And it worked…until it didn’t. One-by-one my friends left me.</p><p> </p><p>After the psychedelic trip, I underwent a years-long healing journey. I read dozens of EQ, therapy, and self-help books. I wrote thousands of pages across my journals. I laughed, I cried, I got in touch with my emotions that I had suppressed my whole life. And then I decided to get strong.</p><p>In the latter half of college, "The House of Iron" became my place of worship—every day I was in the gym at 6am for two hours. In the span of ten months, I went from barely being able to lift a barbell, to deadlifting twice my body weight. I put on 10-15lbs of muscle, and when I looked in the mirror, instead of seeing a scared kid, for the first time in my life I saw the body of a man looking back.</p><p> </p><p>The last time my whole family lived under the same roof was my senior year of college. My brother and I had been getting along for a while, becoming friends for the first time in our lives. I thought there was mutual respect between us. </p><p>I was wrong.</p><p>One morning the dog ran down the hallway loudly, and in the process, accidentally woke up my brother. Watching it happen, I began chuckling at the ridiculousness of it. My brother burst out of his room and saw me quietly snickering. He assumed that I had deliberately woken him up to mess with him, and without asking questions, sprinted towards me and unleashed a barrage of punches. I froze. </p><p>Then after a moment, I realized that his punches didn’t hurt. Not anymore. I had gotten stronger since the last time he struck me. </p><p>He kept hitting me and accusing me, “You woke me up! You woke me up!” I grabbed both of his arms, stopping the assault, and told him, “It was the dog! It was the dog! The dog accidentally woke you up, not me!” He stopped thrashing, stared at me with suspicion, then shrugged and went into the kitchen to go make coffee.</p><p>I just stood there, stunned. Then I went into my room and closed the door. Five minutes later I joined my first Zoom class for the day. During that call, I silently processed what had just happened. I felt confused, shocked, angry.</p><p>After my class, I went looking for my brother, and found him in the backyard by the pool on a lounge chair sunbathing in nothing but his boxers, peacefully reading a book.</p><p>“Hey,” I called to him.</p><p>He casually turned his head towards me.</p><p>“Like I said this morning,” I stated in a cool, even tone while maintaining intense, unblinking eye contact. “It was the dog that woke you up, not me. And without thinking or asking clarifying questions, you attacked me. Look at me,” I demanded while pointing at my eyes. “I’m bigger than you now. I’m stronger than you now. And if you ever put hands on me again, I will <i><strong>fuck </strong></i>you up. There will be blood.” </p><p>He looked at me and saw that I wasn’t fucking around. I dialed my intensity down, smiled, and then told him, “have a nice day.”</p><p>I wasn’t a bystander, not anymore.</p><hr /><p>The man was conflicted. He wanted to continue kicking this woman, but he also saw the bloodlust in my eyes. He tried reasoning with me:</p><p>“She pepper-sprayed me!”</p><p>“I don’t give a <i><strong>FUCK</strong></i>,” I replied. “Get the <i><strong>FUCK</strong></i> away from her.” </p><p>The man teetered on his backfoot, unsure of what to do. And while he was doing that, I was mentally preparing how I was going to incapacitate him.</p><hr /><p>Had my half-naked sunbathing brother actually gotten up to fight, it would have been ugly. Neither of us are trained fighters. But ultimately, the threat of me fighting back for the first time was enough to deter all future conflicts. He gained a kind of respect for me that day. While we've had our ups and downs in the years since then, today we’ve never been closer as brothers. He's a good guy.</p><p>But had we fought that day, it would have been a mess of sprawling limbs. But that wouldn’t be the case today because…<br /> </p><p>…Last year I made a new friend at a coffee shop—an older gentleman. What an amazing and wise individual. In his youth, he was a hothead like me, and he also grew up with a brother that beat him.</p><p>But unlike me, as a young adult he got into dozens of street fights and learned how to defend himself through trial by fire.</p><p>One day, months into our friendship, he asked me, “Do you want to learn how to fight?”</p><p>“Yes.”</p><p>And thus began my training.</p><p> </p><p>Punches. Kicks. Blocks. And also the psychology of fighting—people, especially untrained fighters, are predictable. The most common punch thrown from the untrained fighter is the overhead haymaker. My friend taught me how to block that—turns out that the wax on/wax off technique from <i>Karate Kid </i>actually works—and then how to counter with a series of devastating blows. </p><p>An older man who instills wisdom, philosophy, and the means to defend myself. I had met my Mr. Miyagi.</p><p>At my gym, I began using a punching bag without any gloves to toughen my hands. The first time I did bag work, I whiffed a roundhouse kick and landed hard on my ass. I also walked away with nasty bruises up and down my shins, arms, and fingers. </p><p>But over time I got tougher. When I would visit my friend, he would surprise attack me to try to catch me off guard. And gradually, my reaction time got better.</p><p>Over the course of a few months, I developed a newfound confidence when walking around downtown. It felt like I was armed. And I was. My body became acutely primed to defend itself. I was now ready if a fight ever went down…</p><hr /><p><i>When he charges</i>, I thought, <i>I’ll block the first punch, and counter with an elbow strike to his ear with all my force to go for a knockout blow</i>.</p><p>I took a step forward, my eyes subconsciously communicating: <i>please, enter the arena.</i></p><p>The man took a step back, scowled, and slinked away from the woman he was attacking.</p><p>I followed him.</p><hr /><p>I lived with my parents for a few years after college to save money. Before I moved out, I had one goal.</p><p>After dinner one night, I announced to my parents, “I’m pretty emotionally fucked up. And so are both of you. I’m going to singlehandedly carry this family’s trauma and stress on my back until we’re all healed.” They mocked me for making such a grandiose declaration, but I didn’t care. I didn’t know how I was going to fix us, but I was fully resolved in my ability to do so.</p><p>As I read more books and had emotionally challenging conversations with friends, I took what I learned and practiced at home. One night in particular stood out to me.</p><p>I don’t remember what they were arguing about, but it didn’t matter. What mattered was that my father was yelling at my mother. And I was fucking pissed about it.</p><p>“HEY,” I exclaimed.</p><p>My parents looked at me, probably expecting me to complain about the noise they were making when I was trying to work. Instead, I furiously pointed an accusatory finger at my father and loudly asserted, “<i>You’re not going to yell at my mother again.</i>”</p><p>My father looked at me with a blank expression.<br /> </p><p>We all just stood there in silence.<br /> </p><p>I narrowed my eyes. “Got it?”</p><p>My father, not breaking eye contact with me, said “yeah” then walked away.</p><p>Had my father—who is bigger, taller, and stronger—challenged me and tried to intimidate me, I would have had no choice but to back down. Maybe he saw the resolve in my eyes, and in a moment of self-reflection, realized how he was treating his wife. In that moment, perhaps he overcame the rough childhood he endured and chose peace.</p><p>I wish I could say that everything immediately got better after that night. But it didn’t. Lots of hard conversations ensued over that year. Tears were shed. Yelling.</p><p>I made plenty of mistakes, too, allowing my ego to get the better of me. The road was rocky, but I ultimately kept my promise: I carried my family out of the dregs of our emotional abyss by repeatedly modeling good behavior, choosing the right time to initiate difficult conversations, and believing that I could even do this in the first place.</p><p>Today, my parents and I have never been closer. I’m proud of them, and my brother. They all put the work in to become better versions of themselves, and they deserve the credit for that. They're not perfect (far from it), but they've come a long way.</p><hr /><p>It’s been seven years since the last time I lost control, when the psychedelic demon took over my mind. And now I’m following an attacker.</p><p>The man, sporting the craziest of eyes, was huffing and puffing, “She started it! That crazy bitch pepper-sprayed me!”</p><p>“<i><strong>FUCK</strong></i> off,” I fumed.</p><p>The man walked alongside the group of dads I’d passed earlier and tried to fraternize with them, “That bitch is crazy! Did you see that?? She’s crazy!” The dad closest to him nodded sympathetically with a half-smile to appease him.</p><p>I continued following the man up the street. Any time he turned around to gripe about the woman, I shouted, “<i><strong>FUCK</strong></i> off.” We got to the next street intersection and I watched him walk away while muttering to himself. </p><p>I wasn’t a bystander, not anymore.</p><p> </p><p>When he got a far enough distance away and appeared to not be returning, I remembered what I was doing that night—I was on a date.</p><p>I turned around. My date was watching me. She was eating a cookie.</p><hr /><p>Weeks later, I sat down to write this story alone in my apartment. Remembering everything that happened that day, I began to relive the experience. My heart started beating out of my chest, I broke into a sweat. I felt so, so fucking angry.</p><p>What I still didn’t understand was: on a crowded street, a man was openly assaulting a woman, yet people just watched and moved along—why did no one else help? While writing this story, I sat back in my chair and stared out the window, waiting for the answer to come to me. </p><p>And then it did.</p><p> </p><p> </p><p><i>The man, and the woman he was attacking, were both homeless.</i></p><p> </p><p> </p><p>It’s been over two years since I last cried. </p><p>Sitting at my desk thinking about all this, overwhelmed with emotion, I began to open-mouth weep. I covered my face in shame (because men aren’t supposed to cry), I recognized how silly this was given that I was alone in my apartment, gargled a wet laugh at the irony, then I wept.</p><p>I was thinking of <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sCWe5RRvSHQMccd2Q/i-would-have-shit-in-that-alley-too#Crazy_people_talk_to_themselves_because_no_one_else_will"><u>Teresa</u></a>.</p><p> </p><p> </p><hr /><p>Part 1: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sCWe5RRvSHQMccd2Q/i-would-have-shit-in-that-alley-too">I would have shit in that alley, too</a><br />Part 2: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rLtf5mzsGhgEA75WM/a-city-within-a-city">A City Within a City</a><br />Part 3: <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pykTRvFuSvKGhC8JP/assaulted">Assaulted</a><br />Final Part: Not just a bystander, not anymore…</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jcoXnm6tP4zAA4gjR/not-just-a-bystander-not-anymore#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Oldest Text in the Training Data](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CzZR4ZcJoCNi2zqFn/the-oldest-text-in-the-training-data)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the oldest text in Claude’s training data? Claude was trained on a non-trivial fraction of everything humans have ever written down. That includes the things you’d expect, like books and research papers. But it also includes microfilm archives, transcribed Betamax tapes, Donald Trump’s prenups, ancient scrolls and Harry Potter fanfiction. It’s a weird library.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what is the oldest text in Claude's training data? It mostly depends on what we mean by "text". Here are some candidates:</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Epic of Gilgamesh</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Epic of Gilgamesh is considered the world's oldest written "great work of literature"; the earliest versions of it were written in 2100 BC in Sumer. The Sumerians did a fair amount of writing. Their medium was clay tablets. These tablets were designed to endure a few seasons of the Mesopotamian weather; this turned out to generalize well, and instead of surviving for a few years, they survived for millennia. Gilgamesh was lost in the sands of Iraq for a while, but was eventually dug up by European archeologists. The scholar who first translated the Epic to English got so excited by it that he took all of his clothes off and ran around the English museum shouting "I am the first man to read that after more than two thousand years of oblivion."</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794705963/lexical_client_uploads/xpk3ts7app9tp6lytwu4.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tablets being scanned</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the 1980s and 90s, scholars digitized many of the dug-up clay tablets. The museums would scan the tablets in normal office scanners and fax them to Assyriologists to phonetically transliterate. Those transliterations were somewhat lossy, so the major Mesopotamian scripts were eventually encoded in the Unicode system. Some of the largest Unicode characters come from Sumerian. E.g.:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">𒐫 - is the character for the number 32,400</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">𒈙 - likely means rival kings or a battle between kings.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each one of these is a single unicode character! They are great when you are doing QA or trying to make a website really ugly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anyways, Claude knows those languages well. In particular, it knows the Epic of Gilgamesh very well and can recite it more or less line by line in both Akkadian Unicode and in English.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kushim’s Tablet</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Epic of Gilgamesh is famous for being good and interesting, not just for being old. There are many Sumerian texts that are far older. The Sumerian writing system was developed to keep track of debt and help with commodity logistics. It took them about 700 years to realize that writing was a universal information encoding system. They then started using it to keep track of love songs, jokes and epic stories.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The oldest texts are accounting entries from the administrative archives of Uruk, written between 3400 and 3000 BC, ~5000 years before the "current year of our Claude". We can’t say which tablet is the very oldest: they are dated by archaeological layer and handwriting style, so each one comes with a centuries-wide confidence interval. The most famous of them is the "Kushim Tablet".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The "Kushim Tablet" says something like: "exchange 29,086 measures of barley, 37 months, Kushim’s final account". Kushim is likely the name of the person who received the barley. We believe that Kushim turned the barley into beer. What else would you do with so much barley? Conservatively, this much barley is equivalent to about 900 cans of beer a day. From this, we can infer that Kushim was likely a very chill guy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude can quote the "Kushim Tablet" in Sumerian Unicode. The tablet is definitely in the training data.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794705962/lexical_client_uploads/oepqq6igzvr7xnehp6bb.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kushim's Tablet</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Cone Token</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Writing in Mesopotamia evolved from an older accounting system made of clay tokens. Each token stood for one unit: a cone was a small measure of grain, a sphere was a big measure of food. E.g., if you owed someone 23 measures, the record was three cones and two spheres (10s). The oldest tokens are about 10,000 years old. The tokens predate cities or the wheel.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At some point, ancient accountants started sealing the tokens inside of envelopes made from clay, a way to lock the information in a tamper-proof way. This method had one problem: you couldn’t check what was inside an envelope without smashing it. So the accountants pressed the tokens into the wet surface before sealing them in. Eventually someone noticed that the dents made the tokens redundant. This is where Mesopotamian text comes from.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="MS 4632 - The Schoyen Collection" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794705962/lexical_client_uploads/nqwiejr2jyfvqabcvx0g.jpg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you press a cone into wet clay you get a small wedge. That wedge became the sign for "1"; this is what it looks like in Unicode: "𒁹". Claude knows the "𒁹" character very well, so the oldest text in Claude’s training data might be the cone token (or at least some evolution of it). Interestingly, writing began when we turned tokens into text. Claude reads by turning text into tokens.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CzZR4ZcJoCNi2zqFn/the-oldest-text-in-the-training-data#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Embryo selection and economic discounting](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8Y3DB9qAhxxHnegiJ/embryo-selection-and-economic-discounting)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My wife and I are planning to have children soon, and we will be using embryo selection to improve our family’s future well-being. Basically, the idea is that we can take a small sample of cells from each embryo, analyze their DNA, and use the genetic information to help choose which embryos we want to implant.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These days, polygenic scores are good at predicting risk for a lot of diseases, like cancer, heart disease, diabetes, schizophrenia, and Alzheimer’s. And companies like </span><a href="http://herasight.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Herasight</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://www.lifeview.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Genomic Prediction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> often give an “embryo health score”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo5ywsl1edpg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno5ywsl1edpg">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that takes an average of the predicted risk for each disease, weighted by how bad each disease is.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But how are those weights calculated? And can we do better?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Disability-adjusted life years</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Diseases are bad in two ways: they can make you die, and they can make your life worse. The “disability-adjusted life year” (DALY) metric captures both of these, by combining years of life lost and years of reduced quality of life (using a factor between 0 and 1 to account for different subjective severity of disease states).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn7ou8r37hw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn7ou8r37hw">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embryo selection companies, who compute polygenic scores for many different diseases, can add up the DALY burden of all the disease predictions for a given embryo. This gives an overall “embryo health score” that is a composite of these predictions.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8Y3DB9qAhxxHnegiJ/dwyhc2yarogcm1f0hozv" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DALY scores for five of our embryos, shown against the possible distribution of embryos given the parents’ DNA. Note the left-tailed skew. This is because an increasing genetic risk for a condition causes a nonlinearly increasing risk of having the condition.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A note on pleiotropy</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interestingly, many negative traits are genetically correlated with each other, so selecting against one selects against many. For example, our embryos that had a high schizophrenia risk also tended to have a high risk of bipolar. Pleiotropy (where one variant affects multiple traits) is often brought up in objections to embryo selection, e.g. “if you select for IQ, you might be selecting against health”, but in practice, mutations that make embryos less healthy will also generally hurt other traits.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which diseases will be relevant in the future?</span></h1><p><a href="https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/hus/topics/heart-disease-deaths.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From as far back as 1950 until today, heart disease has been the biggest killer in the USA</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And the DALY scores for embryos reflect this: heart disease has a strong weight.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But if a boy is born in 2027 with a high lifetime risk, he likely won’t develop any problems until middle age. Heart disease treatment will likely look very different in 2072! Therefore, we need to take into account future medical progress when weighing the badness of different diseases:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, some things are already easily treatable. Statins lower cholesterol, and there are plenty of good medications for blood pressure. More recently, GLP1 drugs have made type 2 diabetes much less relevant. Some of these drugs are expensive today, but they will be generic in the future, and likely cheap and accessible.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the other limb bud, any disease that is developmental in origin will not be treatable except by replacement of the affected structure. If something doesn’t form properly in the fetus, giving a drug to the adult won’t help.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, organ replacement will probably be an option in 10-30 years, depending on the organ. This could include xenotransplantation or lab-grown “applied developmental biology” approaches. So things like heart disease and kidney disease will be curable, though this may be very expensive.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But nobody can get brain transplants.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2tgnw82aazz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2tgnw82aazz">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Anything that affects the brain (depression, mental illness, neurodegeneration) will still be a huge problem. Very advanced technology may be able to replace the brain neuron-by-neuron, but I expect this to have the least progress compared to other diseases. Conditions that affect photoreception (e.g. colorblindness) will also be difficult to treat.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cancer and autoimmune conditions are also not treatable with transplants, though emerging technologies will make treatment somewhat better.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, I expect rapid medical progress in most areas over the next 50 years, especially given ongoing advances in AI. And if there’s a technological singularity, and everyone uploads their minds to run on silicon, then mental illnesses and personality disorders are the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> thing that will matter!</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Economic discounting</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leaving aside which conditions will be treatable in the future, the fact that some conditions have later average age of onset than others means that DALYs need to be adjusted for time-preference. For example, schizophrenia usually begins in early adulthood, whereas Alzheimer’s is a disease of old age. Selection for intelligence would have benefits at all stages of life.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp76rjn9iww"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp76rjn9iww">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embryo selection companies don’t take time-discounting into account for their embryo health scores.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxketowrvt4p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxketowrvt4p">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> However, health economists (e.g. in </span><a href="https://idsihealth.org/resources/time-horizon/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a study sponsored by the Gates Foundation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) typically use a 3% annual discount rate. Personally, I think a 3% rate is on the low side: money invested today (instead of spent on embryo selection) would likely grow faster than that, especially given AI progress, and could be used to treat health conditions later in life. But I’ll use 3% in this post. Applied to the question of embryo selection, economic discounting means that late-onset conditions should be weighted less.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DALY adjustments</span></h1><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8Y3DB9qAhxxHnegiJ/p1ywelnzlqolihqknz5j" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">*IQ is valued through earnings (</span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8146900/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grosse &amp; Zhou 2021</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, updated to 2026 dollars). †BMI is accounted for through the diseases it causes and has no weight on its own.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are Herasight’s DALY numbers,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffvwsw0m5vf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfvwsw0m5vf">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> adjusted for 3% economic discounting, and further adjusted for future medical progress. I assume negligible progress in mental and neurological disorders, moderate progress in cancer and autoimmune, and faster progress in other categories (especially ones which are related to obesity/metabolic dysfunction).</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even after discounting, embryo selection is still worth it</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For couples doing IVF, embryo selection (PGT) costs vary by provider but are often in the range of $500 per embryo.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefinfsl0qtnq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fninfsl0qtnq">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> IVF itself is much more expensive: we paid about $7500 per cycle at CNY, including costs of medication and travel, and other clinics are even more expensive. Side effects of hormone stimulation for egg retrieval also take a toll: based on my wife’s experience, I estimate that these side effects cause a loss of 0.025 DALY per IVF cycle.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref73cfg556jn2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn73cfg556jn2">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But all this is still much less expensive than the diseases that selection can prevent. As of 2026, one DALY is valued </span><a href="https://aspe.hhs.gov/sites/default/files/documents/2d83af5823915d81871334ee08ad03d9/Standard-RIA-Values-2026.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">by the US government (HHS)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">$726,000 … for analyses using a 3% discount rate, and $1,205,000 for analyses using a 7% discount rate</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv1o2gceu31"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv1o2gceu31">[9]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other US healthcare organizations place lower values on DALYs, often around $200,000. The difference is because the HHS number measures what people will pay to reduce their own risk of death, but other organizations are measuring cost-effectiveness of medical interventions relative to current treatment options.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The top embryo from our first IVF cycle had a non-discounted DALY gain of +1.35 years, and estimated IQ gain of +10 points, relative to our family average. Economic discounting and medical progress would shrink the DALY gain to +0.18 years, but this is still a gain of $133,000 for health and $151,000 for IQ, much more than we paid for the IVF and selection.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course we can’t be sure that our top embryo will successfully implant and develop to term, so we need to scale these numbers down to account for the chance we need to use our second-best or lower ranked embryos instead. In the end, being able to choose among the five embryos from our first cycle, rather than transferring them in random order, has an expected value of about $112,000.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additional cycles run into diminishing returns, but I calculated that this point is reached for our family after 6 - 8 cycles.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8Y3DB9qAhxxHnegiJ/sh9xbng0ooxv7ymp9bon" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here, the optimal number of cycles is calculated as expected benefit (from health only, or health + IQ) with current Herasight predictors, minus the expected cost (direct financial cost and DALYs lost from IVF medication side effects).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6h5d43vgmyt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6h5d43vgmyt">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our family is different from a typical family in several ways, including higher genetic risk of bipolar and schizophrenia, and younger than average age for IVF patients (meaning more embryos per cycle).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For typical families, embryo selection for health is worth it only at the highest estimates of DALY values. However, the picture changes when also considering IQ selection: typical families would come out ahead from doing at least 3 rounds of IVF, and interestingly, lower value placed on DALYs </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">increases </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the optimal IVF cycle number, because the DALY cost of medication side effects becomes less relevant.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusions</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given rate of progress in medicine, and economic growth, embryo selection to prevent early-onset disease is more important than selection to prevent late-onset disease. Prospective parents who are considering embryo selection should take this into account. Even with discounting, embryo selection for health+IQ still passes the cost-benefit analysis for most couples.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fno5ywsl1edpg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo5ywsl1edpg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Embryo health score” is Genomic Prediction’s term; Herasight calls this “polygenic longevity index”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn7ou8r37hw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn7ou8r37hw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Calculation of these factors involves surveying patients and the general public about their preferences (e.g. would you rather live a year with blindness or with Crohn’s disease?), and involves a lot of statistics which I won’t get into here.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2tgnw82aazz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2tgnw82aazz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If someone got a “brain transplant” it would actually be a “body transplant”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp76rjn9iww"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp76rjn9iww">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Economically, we can quantify this by the effect of IQ on future income. Grosse and Zhou estimated this as $10,600 per point (on the low end) in 2016 dollars. Adjusting for inflation, that would be about $15,000 today. As of this writing, Genomic Prediction does not offer intelligence selection but Herasight does.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxketowrvt4p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxketowrvt4p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked a representative of Herasight about this, who said that they are considering adding an optional economic discounting feature in a future update, but haven’t formalized a framework yet.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfvwsw0m5vf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffvwsw0m5vf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This combines years of life lost and years lived with disability. Herasight also uses different weights for male and female embryos, since women tend to live longer and some diseases affect women and men at different base rates. The table shows the average.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fninfsl0qtnq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefinfsl0qtnq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some companies like Herasight charge a fixed fee for analysis of any number of embryos, though biopsy fees also need to be paid to the IVF clinics.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn73cfg556jn2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref73cfg556jn2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My wife’s experience was about as bad as a month of major depression for each cycle (30% loss of quality of life for a month). I’m very grateful for her going through this.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv1o2gceu31"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv1o2gceu31">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They get these two different numbers by back-calculating from $14.1M as the statistical value of an average adult life based on surveys; i.e., an average American would be indifferent between losing $14,100 or having an 0.1% chance of death. Also, these numbers are technically QALYs not DALYs, though the difference is minor. Finally, for effects occurring in future years, they say to raise the value by 1.117% per year due to future real income growth, which partially offsets the discounting.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6h5d43vgmyt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6h5d43vgmyt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These calculations depend on: the expected number of euploid embryos per cycle (3 for typical IVF patients, 5 for us), the live birth rate per euploid embryo (60% for typical IVF patients, 67% for us), and genetic risk (population average vs. our specific risks).</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8Y3DB9qAhxxHnegiJ/embryo-selection-and-economic-discounting#comments">Discuss</a>

## AI/ML

### [Understanding Positional Embeddings in Modern LLMs](https://magazine.sebastianraschka.com/p/understanding-positional-embeddings)

**来源**: Sebastian Raschka

**摘要**: The Methods Behind How LLMs Represent Token Positions

---

## Engineering

### [Bliki: Generated Glossary](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/GeneratedGlossary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>When we use LLMs in a specialized domain, such as the operations of any
  reasonably-large organization, there is a lot of specialized vocabulary. Such
  words and specialized meanings will be foreign to an LLM because they aren't
  in its training set, making it harder for the LLM to be effective when doing
  tasks in that domain. One technique to aid this is to use an LLM to generate a
  glossary of the terminology in that domain, and make this glossary available
  for any LLMs doing work in those circumstances.</p>

<p>The sources for the LLM can be any material within the organization
  concerned such as documents, emails, and chat logs. In some cases getting
  transcripts of audio or video may be helpful, although most organizations
  generate enough text to do the job.</p>

<p>The glossary-generating LLM should look for single words, and phrases of
  several words that regularly hang out together to form a meaningful term. The
  generator should be asked to identify terms that are new to it, but also known
  terms that are being used in unfamiliar ways. The latter can often cause the
  most confusion.</p>

<p>Once we have a list of specialized vocabulary we can ask the agent to
  produce a glossary, with definitions for each term. Some terms may
  be used in different ways, in which case the LLM should provide alternative
  definitions, with a note as to what context the differences appear in. The LLM
  can be asked to follow the output format of a dictionary, with a focus on
  precision and conciseness.</p>

<p>Once we have a draft glossary, then  human experts in that domain should
  review it, and amend it for cases where the LLM has got the wrong ends of
  sticks.</p>

<p>We then put the glossary somewhere where any other LLM can use it. It
  should be in a form that's easy for the LLM to consult, and minimize the
  tokens the LLM should consume in order to find meaning. We should set up a
  schedule to review and refresh the glossary at regular intervals, by agents
  and humans. We should keep it in version control, so we can see how it changes
  over time.</p>

<p>Large organizations often have subtly different definitions in different
  places, essentially following the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain Driven Design</a> notion
  of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">Bounded Context</a>. Source code can be a particularly
  interesting bounded context, allowing folks to understand how terminology is
  used within a code base. Sometimes a single glossary can span several of
  bounded contexts, but at other times it's better to have separate glossaries
  for each one, allowing LLMs to consult whichever glossaries that are relevant
  to what they are doing. I'd urge everyone to resist any temptation to create a
  single, standard, glossary - I've yet to see that work. Often the value of a
  glossary isn't to nail down a single definition of a term, but instead to
  highlight its different usage in different contexts.</p>

<p>When there are different bounded contexts it can be interesting to compare
  different glossaries, or to get LLMs to compare glossaries and describe
  differences in usage. Comparing a source-code derived glossary to a human text
  can be particularly instructive.</p>

<p>If the glossary is large, it can be worthwhile to only include the terms
  and links to definitions as part of the initial context. The model can then
  use the links for only the full definitions it needs while working.</p>

<p>A glossary can be used in several ways within <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">Agentic Engineering</a>. Glossaries can inform generation of code, so that
  names within the generated software fit the particular vocabulary of the
  enterprise. <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">Sensors in a
  harness</a> can check for this usage, both in the internals of the software
  and in the user-interface.</p>

<p>When generating code or documents, we may introduce new bounded contexts.
  For example, internal names that may be used within an organization should not
  be exposed in a customer-facing UI, because such internal terms are likely to be
  only confuse a customer. In this case a customer has a different bounded
  context to the part of the enterprise serving them.</p>

<p>It's often useful for glossaries to include terms that should not be used,
  in which case it includes advice on what other term to use instead. Terms that
  are deprecated can be handled in this way, with glossary entries that indicate
  what period of time a term should be in effect.</p>

<p>In my conversations with people working with agents in large organizations,
  a common theme is the difficulty of specialized language. This encourages the
  activities of domain modeling, building ontologies, and constructing explicit
  <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html#context-layer">context
  layers in enterprise data</a>. Getting LLMs to generate a glossary is a
  simple, but useful, part of this work.</p>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Eric Nagler and Unmesh Joshi related their experiences doing this
    activity and thus inspired this post. Amy Pellegrini, Annie Luxton, Arun Srinivasan, Chris Ford, Daniel
    Terhorst-North, Dave Farley, Eric Nagler, James Shore, Jason Yip, Jimmy Nilsson, Matteo Vaccari, Michaël Le Barbier, Pete Hodgson, Pramod
    Sadalage, Ravi Vats, Unmesh Joshi, and Valentyn Logvynskyi commented on drafts.
    </p>
</div>

### [Tests as data: ensuring AI-generated code does what we want](https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Automated testing in the age of AI coding assistants is problematic:
     when AI agents write both production and test code, developers risk losing
     the assurance that automated tests protect against functional errors.
     <b class="author">Matteo Vaccari</b> makes three claims: (1) when AI writes the
     code, developers become its customers; (2) therefore customer tests, not
     developer tests, become the primary contract between humans and AI; (3)
     moving that contract into test data rather than test code makes it easier
     to protect it from unexpected modifications by coding agents. All of this
     optimizes for human-readable tests that establish a contract between humans
     and AI. While in the past DSL parsers and custom test runners were
     expensive to write and maintain, now AI makes them cheap.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: November 10](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-10.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>It’s that time of the year when the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">November Thoughtworks Technology Radar</a> appears. Again my colleagues have taken a sweep of the technologies that our teams have used in the field, and pulled together a summary of what’s popped up in the last six months. It’s been a busy half-year, one that again has been dominated by AI, and that’s reflected in the many AI blips on the radar.</p>

<p>The mass of blips makes it hard to develop a coherent story around trends, but Chris Ford has done a fine job of the writing the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/blog/technology-strategy/macro-trends-tech-industry-november-2026">accompanying article on macro trends</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One team of passionate people experimenting with agents can make almost anything work. They share a context, a credit card and a mental model. They review each other’s work because they sit together, and they know what to check and how much latitude to give the coding agent.</p>

  <p>Two hundred people, though, cannot work that way. The moment agentic AI becomes an operating model rather than a pilot, a second layer of problems appears: How is context shared between teams? What’s the token bill? How do we deal with increased review demands? What’s the agent allowed to reach, read and change? And who’s now responsible for the harness everyone depends on?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Experience so far suggests that the questions that enterprises face are about: shared context, token cost, cognitive load, security, and platform building. While these issues are distinct, they are not separate, so strategies to answer them must take all of them into account. I also echo Chris in reminding us that however impressive these tools are, we must remember that while we have to rethink systems and workflows to utilize these technologies, they should be built to serve humans - and we should all benefit from its bounty.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ben Thompson’s Stratechery newsletter <a href="https://stratechery.com/">reflects on  the recent election’s impact on data centers</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I watch a lot of sports, and particularly this fall, watched a lot of baseball. My Milwaukee Brewers — excuse me, my World Champion Milwaukee Brewers — demanded my full attention, which also meant I saw a lot of campaign ads, and by far the most common topic, at least here in Wisconsin, was data centers. Moreover, it was never entirely clear which way it cut politically: both parties were anti-data center, accusing their opponent of being the opposite.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that this opposition is recent, and tied to a shift in focus from the internet and streaming movies to AI:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>you know, the technology that in the best case scenario is going to take everyone’s jobs, and in the worst case scenario end the human race.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s going to be a lot said about how Democratic officials will be at the heart of blocking data center building, but as his point above about campaign ads illustrates, the opposition to data centers is (at least at the moment) a bi-partisan opposition.</p>

<p>He follows up with “ten takes” from these consequences. The big beneficiaries of this opposition to building data centers will be those who own the data centers that have already been built. The opposition will reduce supply of tokens, meaning that token costs will go up, thus increasing the profits for those who already have their token generators plugged in and humming.</p>

<p>There’s also benefits to those who aren’t constrained by the need to build more big data centers. Other countries aren’t affected by a change in US policy, China is the most talked-about case here, but it’s not the only country that may benefit from a drop in US data center building. Apple may also benefit, if it means more AI work is pushed to edge devices that are their territory, in addition to having not sunk so many dollars into AI-specific investments.</p>

<p>To his analysis, I’ll add that this may also have a somewhat counter-intuitive effect on regulation, and corporate attitudes to it. The common expectation is that businesses are opposed to government regulation, since that interferes with their free pursuit of profit. That’s true to an extent, but it’s quite common to see established businesses lobby (quietly) in favor of regulation that acts as a barrier to new competitors. So I wouldn’t be at all surprised to see companies that have already built data centers to be pushing for regulations that impede or prevent new ones from being built.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve been a long-time fan of Hadley Wickham, for how he has tamed the rather messy world of R programming into a <a href="https://tidyverse.org/">well-designed tidyverse</a>. He is another thoughtful voice to listen to as we move into the world of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a>, in this case by trying to figure out <a href="https://substack.hadley.nz/p/how-much-do-tokens-really-cost">what the real cost of tokens is</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I hear a lot of suspicion that AI tokens are unsustainably priced and that frontier labs are heavily subsidising costs with their VC money. They want to get us hooked now, so they can pull the rug out from under us whenever they want to make a bunch of money. Is that true? My sense is that it’s mostly not. But can I back that up with some data?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His conclusion is that it doesn’t seem that token costs are significantly subsidized on per-token API basis, but they are with subscription plans. (He caveats his analysis, saying it doesn’t take into account recouping the costs of training the models.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There’s a creepy excitement at the thought that models are clever enough to edit the weights of models, an activity that <a href="https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/">Takehi et al refer to as Weight-Space Programming</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While we don’t know if we’ll ever be able to download skills directly into our brains (<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fhrNgXJ__n8">“I know kung fu”</a> - or for those in my particular demographic: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_90">Joe 90</a>), this does raise the possibility of downloading capabilities into models by manipulating their weights.</p>

<p>Simon Wilison riffed on this paper by <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Nov/3/qwen-can-interpret-model-weights/">examining whether an LLM can understand model behaviors from the weights alone</a>, by examining four simple neural networks, each trained to play a specific game, and figuring out which game it was by just looking at the weights.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/jessitron/status/2117400669044793405">Jessica Kerr (jessitron):</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>My days of teaching AI to do stuff are numbered.</p>

  <p>Getting it to teach me stuff is more fun.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Houck et al (that “al” includes some folks who I’ve learned to pay a lot of attention to), look at how we can assess the quality of context supplied to agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>An agent may draw from source code, specifications, issue trackers, chat histories, architectural documents, tool output, and user instructions, then act on that combination with limited human supervision. Information that was written for different audiences, at different times, and for different purposes is brought together into a temporary operating environment for a particular task. Whether each source is individually useful does not reveal whether the resulting context is fit for the work the agent is being asked to do.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>They’ve come up with a <a href="https://queue.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3847288">framework for describing the quality of this assembled context</a>, which they refer to as CAFE(S). The acronym refers to five dimensions they look at:</p>

<ul>
  <li>Clarity: Can the agent interpret the request as intended?</li>
  <li>Actionability: Can the agent proceed, and know when it is done?</li>
  <li>Fidelity: Can the agent trust that this context is true?</li>
  <li>Efficiency: Can the agent focus on what matters?</li>
  <li>Security: Should the agent have this context at all?</li>
</ul>

<p>They also list a set of half-a-dozen “context smells”: common cases where these break down (and a nod to Kent’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/CodeSmell.html">original metaphor</a>)</p>

<p>I’m not sure about the name, (how exactly should I pronounce “CAFE(S)”?) and the article exhibits rather more <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a> that I prefer. But on the whole I think this is a good framing for thinking about an important part of feeding our silicon servants/overlords.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Cindy Sridharan noted that <a href="https://x.com/copyconstruct/status/2118529738532925725">the problems she ran into six months ago are no longer around</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What was missing in my setup 6 months ago was the verification process I now use.</p>

  <p>70% of my daily token spend goes into verifying patches, and only ~20-30% into creating them.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That resonated with conversations I’ve had with folks using agents in enterprise settings. They are using far more tokens to check the working of models (through guardrails and evals) than they are to generate a response to a request. A single request to a model from a customer may trigger a dozen turns to other models to probe its input and verify its output.</p>

---

### [Long-horizon agents and durable execution](https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Long-horizon agents can automate complex tasks that take days or weeks
     to complete and involve multiple exchanges with people and external
     systems. Many things can go wrong during this time, including process
     crashes, network failures, and unanticipated real-world behavior.
     <b class="author">Kiran Prakash</b> explains durable execution: an emerging
     pattern that helps agents recover from these failures and move towards
     their goal. In this programming model, the runtime keeps a record of the
     result of each step that interacts with the outside world so that, when a
     failure occurs, the program runs again, completed steps are skipped, and
     the program's state is rebuilt from the record.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [The Data Product Platform Navigator](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Many organizations adopting data mesh and data products face a common
     challenge. They may be tasked with building a self-service platform for
     data products and data mesh from scratch, or they may have an existing
     platform that suffers from low adoption, preventing their data mesh from
     scaling. This article provides a platform navigator, based on the CNCF's
     Platform Engineering Maturity Model, to help you reason about your current
     situation and determine the next steps for your platform.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html">more…</a></p>
